Field Theory Approach to Classical N N -Particle Systems In and Out of Equilibrium
Tristan Daus, Elena Kozlikin

Abstract
We present an approach to solving the evolution of a classical N N -particle ensemble based on the path integral approach to classical mechanics. This formulation provides a perturbative solution to the Liouville equation in terms of a propagator which can be expanded in a Dyson series. We show that this perturbative expansion exactly corresponds to an iterative solution of the BBGKY-hierarchy in orders of the interaction potential. Using the path integral formulation, we perform a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation (HST) to obtain an effective field theoretic description in terms of macroscopic fields, which contains the full microscopic dynamics of the system in its vertices. Naturally, the HST leads to a new perturbative expansion scheme which contains an infinite order of microscopic interactions already at the lowest order of the perturbative expansion. Our approach can be applied to in and out of equilibrium systems with arbitrary interaction potentials and initial conditions. We show how (unequal-time) cumulants of the Klimontovich phase space densities can be computed within this framework and derive results for density and momentum correlations for a spatially homogeneous system. Under the explicit assumptions for the interaction potential and initial conditions, we show that well-known results related to plasma oscillations and the Jeans instability criterion for gravitational collapse can be recovered in the lowest order perturbative expansion and that both are the effect of the same collective behaviour of the many-body system.



1
Introduction

The overwhelming success of the path integral formulation of quantum field theory had sparked a keen interest in an analogous formulation for classical mechanics more than half a century ago. The pioneering work of
[
1
]
, - now known as the MSR formalism -, laid the groundwork for a path integral formulation for stochastic systems
[
2
]
and for classical Hamiltonian systems
[
3
,
4
]
. The idea behind the introduction of the path integral formalism to classical mechanics was to apply the wealth of already developed functional methods to classical systems
[
5
]
. Perhaps more importantly, the goal was to give to the theory of classical statistical mechanics the same generality and rigour as quantum field theory
[
1
]
. The path integral formulation was, thus, intended to encompass the properties of a system, be it conservative or dissipative, or in or out of equilibrium. It would also provide a graphical representation in terms of Feynman diagrams and it would
allow to apply non-perturbative methods from quantum field theory to classical systems. While the works of
[
1
,
2
,
5
]
have given rise to many applications of the path integral formalism for stochastic systems
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
, not much attention – beside a purely academic interest
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
– has been paid to the path integral formulation for classical Hamiltonian systems introduced by
[
3
,
4
]
. However, there are many applications for the latter, ranging from plasma systems
[
13
]
to cosmic Dark Matter large-scale structures
[
14
,
15
]
. The common denominator for such systems is that typical mean-field approximations or standard fluid descriptions become insufficient for their description when they are dominated by effects that go beyond collective behaviour. To study such systems, numerical
N
-body simulations have become the standard method. These are, however, computationally expensive, especially for systems with long-ranged interactions, and heavily tailored to each particular application. Although, they can successfully reproduce results for those systems they were designed to simulate, they are not suitable as a general framework to gain a fundamental understanding of many-body physics.

To establish such a framework, we thus, return to the original idea of the MSR formalism in order to establish a general and rigorous analytical
approach for the study of classical
N
-particle systems based on the path integral formulation of classical mechanics proposed by
[
3
,
4
]
.
Our main interest is the study of structure formation and evolution in terms of
n
-point correlation functions of the Klimontovich phase space densities. We obtain a perturbative solution to the Liouville equation in terms of a propagator which can be expanded in a Dyson series, which corresponds to an iterative solution of the BBGKY-hierarchy in orders of the interaction potential. This microscopic perturbative expansion is not new. However, the path integral formulation allows us to establish a macroscopic field theory by applying a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation (HST)
[
16
,
17
]
to the generating functional. The HST leads to an effective field theoretic description which contains the full statistics of the microscopic system in its vertices. A similar approach was explored in
[
14
]
.

We set up a new macroscopic perturbation theory in terms of macroscopic propagators and vertices, which corresponds to a re-ordering of the microscopic perturbation theory. The macroscopic propagator contains a re-summation of microscopic terms of a certain class to infinite order in the interaction potential. Furthermore, the macroscopic field theory allows us to apply functional (non-perturbative) methods from (quantum) field theory which we shall discuss in future work. The macroscopic theory is therefore especially interesting for long-ranged interaction potentials where perturbative approaches do not suffice. The approach presented here is completely general, it can be applied to in- and out-of-equilibrium systems for any (two-particle) interaction potential and arbitrary initial conditions.

The contents of this paper are structured in the following way: In Sec.
2.1
we introduce our notation and refresh the readers memory on a few well-known quantities from statistical mechanics that will be used later on. We then derive the path integral for classical mechanics in Sec.
2.2
and discuss the microscopic perturbation theory extensively in Sec.
2.3
. We provide calculations of Klimontovich phase space densities up to second order in perturbation theory and draw the connection to the BBGKY-hierarchy, the Boltzmann and the Vlasov equation. In addition, we provide an intuitive picture of the microscopic perturbation theory, as it will play an important role in the macroscopic field theory, which we derive in Sec.
3
. First, we construct a generating functional and perform a (modified) HST in Sec.
3.1
in order to obtain the macroscopic field theory. We then derive Feynman rules in Sec.
3.2
and discuss the free or tree-level theory and its connection to the microscopic theory in Sec.
3.3
and Sec.
3.4
. Finally, we provide an instructive toy-model application in Sec.
3.5
where we compute the tree-level density and momentum correlations for a spatially homogeneous system. At the end of Sec.
3.5
we recover two well-known effects – one from plasma physics and one from cosmology – in our tree-level theory and show that both are, in fact, the result of the same collective behaviour of a many-body system.

2
Time Evolution of the Classical Ensemble

2.1
Basics and Notation

We denote the microstate of an
N
-particle ensemble in three-dimensional Euclidean space by
the set of phase space coordinates
x_{j}=(\vec{q}_{j},\vec{p}_{j})^{\top}\,,\,\,j=1\cdots N
of the individual particles, with respective canonical positions and momenta
\vec{q}_{j}
,
\vec{p}_{j}
. Furthermore, we assume the Hamilton function of the system to be of the form

H(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)=H_{0}(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)+V(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)\,,

(1)

with

H_{0}(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{{\vec{p}_{i}}^{\,2}}{2m}\,,\quad V(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|,t)\,,

(2)

where
v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|,t)
is a possibly time dependent two-particle interaction potential depending only on the absolute value of the distance of the two particles. Moreover, all particles are assumed to have the same mass
m
. The temporal evolution of the system is now described by the set of phase space trajectories
\{x_{j}(t)\}
of the particles which are subject to Hamilton’s equations of motion

\displaystyle\dot{\vec{q}}_{j}=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{p}_{j}}H(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)

(3)

\displaystyle\dot{\vec{p}}_{j}=

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\vec{q}_{j}}H(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)\,,\,\,\,j=1\cdots N\,

(4)

which can be brought into the symplectic form

\dot{x}_{j}=\omega\cdot\nabla_{j}H(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)\,,

(5)

where
\omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{1}_{3\times 3}\\
-\mathbb{1}_{3\times 3}&0\end{pmatrix}
and
\nabla_{j}=(\nabla_{\vec{q}_{j}},\nabla_{\vec{p}_{j}})^{\top}
are the symplectic matrix and the phase space gradient, respectively. Due to the indistinguishable nature of the particles almost all of the quantities we are interested in will depend on the whole particle ensemble. Thus, in order to increase readability, we introduce a tensorial notation, bundling all objects which carry a particle index, e. g.
q_{j}
, into multiparticle objects,

\boldsymbol{A}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}A_{j}\otimes e_{j}\,,

(6)

where
e_{j}
is the
N
-dimensional canonical unit-vector. The set of phase space coordinates
\{x_{j}\}
is now, for instance, represented by the tensor
\boldsymbol{x}
. Note that from the definition of the tensor product, the scalar product between two quantities
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
decomposes as

\boldsymbol{A\cdot B}=\sum_{j,i=1}^{N}(A_{i}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(B_{j}\otimes e_{j})=\sum_{j,i=1}^{N}A_{i}B_{j}\underbrace{e_{i}\cdot e_{j}}_{=\delta_{ij}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}A_{i}B_{i}\,.

(7)

Functions depending on the whole set of particle positions will be denoted by

F(\{x_{j}\},t)=F(x_{1}\,,\cdots\,,x_{N},t)\equiv F(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,.

(8)

Furthermore, the integration measure of the
N
-particle phase space is written as

\mathrm{d}^{6N}x=\mathrm{d}^{3N}q\,\mathrm{d}^{3N}p\equiv\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,.

(9)

Using this notation, the equations of motion for the whole bundle of particles can be brought into the convenient form

0=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla}H(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,,

(10)

where
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\omega\otimes\mathbb{1}_{N\times N}
is the tensorial generalization of the symplectic structure.

Note, that by the above equation of motion the phase space trajectories travelled by particles are uniquely defined by the Hamilton function once an appropriate set of initial conditions
\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}=\boldsymbol{x}(t=t^{(\mathrm{i})})
at initial time
t^{(\mathrm{i})}
is given. This makes the determinism of classical mechanics manifest.

In general we do not know the initial phase space coordinates explicitly but only on a statistical level, i. e. through an initial phase space probability density distribution

\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\varrho_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(11)

Consequently, the specific final positions of the particles are only known on a probabilistic level, even though the trajectories themselves are deterministic. Drawing different realizations of initial coordinates from
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
and evolving them according to (
5
) will eventually result in a final phase space density distribution
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
. Thus at any given time
t>t^{(\mathrm{i})}
, the probability density of the system is given by

\displaystyle\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

(12)

\displaystyle K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=

\displaystyle\,\,\delta_{D}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\,,

(13)

where
\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})
denotes the classical solution to (
10
) evaluated at time
t
, with parametric dependence on the initial conditions. In the above equation,
K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
can be seen as the
transition probability
from the initial phase space density to the final one. Classical determinism, as discussed above, implies that
K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
is exactly given by the Dirac-Delta distribution in (
13
), ensuring that the particles are found at
\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})
.

A classical, time-independent observable is defined as a phase space function
\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x})
of the
6N
variables. Its value at time
t
, given by
\mathcal{O}(t)=\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x}(t))
, is, by the above discussion, also a random variable. Consequently, its statistical average at time
t
can be computed as

\langle\mathcal{O}(t)\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,.

(14)

Inserting equation (
12
) we find

\langle\mathcal{O}(t)\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x})K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(15)

Integrating the above equation over the phase space coordinates
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
we eventually arrive at an expression for the classical expectation value of
\mathcal{O}
in terms of the initial phase space density

\langle\mathcal{O}(t)\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(16)

This result can be generalized to correlations between operators
\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}
at respective times
t_{1},\cdots t_{k}
,

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(t_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(t_{k})\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\mathcal{O}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t_{1};\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t_{k};\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(17)

For simple systems with analytic solutions
\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})
equation (
17
) gives the prescription on how to compute expectation values. In general, however, the analytical solution to (
5
) is not known, such that we need a different approach. Our main objective in this work will, therefore, be to construct a path integral expression for the transition amplitude (
13
), thereby facilitating a more quantum-like treatment of the
N
-particle statistical system.

2.1.1
Macroscopic Observables

Let us now briefly define the primary observables and statistical objects that will be of interest to us. Given the probability distribution at some time
t
, we are typically interested in the computation of collective properties of the whole
N
-particle ensemble. Let us therefore introduce the (local) Klimontovich phase space density,

f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))\delta_{D}(\vec{{p}}_{1}-\vec{p}_{i}(t_{1}))\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(x_{1}-x_{i}(t_{1}))\,,

(18)

which counts the number of microscopic particles of the ensemble, living in
6N
-dimensional
\Gamma
-space, that occupy a phase space point
{x}_{1}=(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1})
in
6
-dimensional
\mu
-space at time
t_{1}
.
Each particle thus contributes a term
\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))\delta_{D}(\vec{{p}}_{1}-\vec{p}_{i}(t_{1}))
to the local macroscopic phase space density. In the following, we will refer to
{x}=(\vec{{q}},\vec{{p}})
and
x_{i}=(\vec{q}_{i},\vec{p}_{i})
as the macroscopic and microscopic variables respectively. The phase space density is consequently a function of external macroscopic variables
(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1})
and the microscopic variables
\boldsymbol{x}(t_{1})
describing the state of the system at time
t_{1}
. For better readability however, we generally keep the latter dependence implicit as the state of the system is already characterized by the time
t_{1}
. It should also be noted that the index
i
in equation (
18
) is a particle label, while the index
1
attached to the
\mu
-space coordinates of
f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})
refers to the phase space point at which we read off the Klimontovich phase space density. We trust that the appropriate meaning of the indices should become apparent from context. Further observables such as the physical density
\rho(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1})
or the momentum density
\vec{\Pi}(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1})
can be obtained from (
18
) according to

\displaystyle\rho(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{1}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\,,

(19)

\displaystyle\vec{\Pi}(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\vec{p}_{i}(t_{1})\,\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{1}\,\vec{{p}}_{1}\,f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\,.

(20)

In general we will also be interested in higher order correlations of (
19
) and (
20
), connecting different points
\vec{{q}}_{1}\cdots\vec{{q}}_{k}
and times
t_{1}\cdots t_{k}
in configuration space, such as unequal-time two-point density correlations.
We will thus be looking at observables of the form

\mathcal{O}(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1},\cdots\vec{{q}}_{k},t_{k})=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{k}F(\vec{{p}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{{p}}_{k})f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(\vec{{q}}_{k},\vec{{p}}_{k},t_{k})\,,

(21)

where
F(\vec{{p}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{{p}}_{k})
is some function of the momenta.
The collective properties of the system are then captured by taking the average over the microscopic degrees of freedom at the respective times, giving

\langle\mathcal{O}(\vec{{q}}_{1},t_{1},\cdots\vec{{q}}_{k},t_{k})\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{3}{p}_{k}F(\vec{{p}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{{p}}_{k})\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(\vec{{q}}_{k},\vec{{p}}_{k},t_{k})\rangle\,.

(22)

Consequently, our main task reduces to the computation of averages of products of the Klimontovich phase space density, which can be done within the path integral framework.

2.1.2
Reduced phase space densities

For a given phase space density
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
we define the
s
-particle reduced phase space density according to

f_{s}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s},t)\equiv\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}x_{s+1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{3}x_{N}\varrho(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s},x_{s+1},\cdots,x_{N},t)\,.

(23)

It captures the statistical information of an
s
-particle subsystem at time
t
. Depending on the concrete observable to compute we may thus describe the system using the reduced phase space densities
f_{s}
instead of the full phase space statistics
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
which reduces the amount of complexity considerably. Due to the indistinguishable nature of the particles and the resulting symmetry of
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
under the exchange of any two particles, the definition of the reduced densities in (
23
) is equivalent to

f_{s}({x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{s},t)=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\sum_{i_{1}\neq\cdots\neq i_{s}=1}^{N}\delta_{D}({x}_{1}-x_{i_{1}})\cdots\delta_{D}({x}_{s}-x_{i_{s}})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,.

(24)

By equation (
14
) the equal time expectation value of a product of
k
-phase space densities at time
t
is given by

\langle f(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t)\cdots f(\vec{q}_{k},\vec{p}_{k},t)\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}f(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1};\boldsymbol{x})\cdots f(\vec{q}_{k},\vec{p}_{k};\boldsymbol{x})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,,

(25)

where we made the dependence on the microscopic degrees of freedom explicit. Using (
24
), this integral decomposes into reduced densities, such that

\langle f(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t)\cdots f(\vec{q}_{k},\vec{p}_{k},t)\rangle=\cdots+f_{k}({x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{k},t)\,.

(26)

The ellipsis in the above equation stands for all terms which involve reduced phase space densities of degree lower than
k
. They arise from terms in the product of sums in (
25
) in which at least two indices are identified, e. g. for
k=2
we find

\displaystyle\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t)f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t)\rangle=

\displaystyle\,\delta_{D}({x}_{1}-{x}_{2})f_{1}({x}_{1},t)+f_{2}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},t)\,.

(27)

We call the respective terms in the decomposition in (
26
) the
r
-particle contribution with
1\leq r\leq k
to the
k
-point correlation function, as they depend on the statistics of
r
-particle subsets. On an intuitive level, they correspond to the possibility of randomly picking the same particle multiple times in order to measure the
k
-point correlation function. Thus, in order to study the evolution of correlation functions (
26
), it is enough to understand the time-evolution of the
s
-particle densities. In the case of equal-time correlation functions this corresponds to the standard approach leading to the BBGKY hierachy. For comparison to our approach we summarize the key points in appendix
B
.

If our system is in an uncorrelated state, i. e. the particles are distributed in a statistically independent way sucht that the probability of finding a particle at some point is independent of any of the other particles, the
s
-particle reduced distribution function takes the form

f_{s}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s},t)=\prod_{j=1}^{s}f_{1}(x_{j},t)\,.

(28)

In order to measure how much the system differs from such an uncorrelated state, we decompose a set of
s
-particles into all possible disjoint subsets which contain at least one particle. Each such cluster represents a group of mutually correlated particles. The clusters themselves are statistically independent from each other such that we can write, for instance,

\displaystyle f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2},t)=

\displaystyle f_{1}(x_{1},t)f_{1}(x_{2},t)+g_{2}(x_{1},x_{2},t)\,,

(29)

\displaystyle f_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},t)=

\displaystyle f_{1}(x_{1},t)f_{1}(x_{2},t)f_{1}(x_{3},t)+f_{1}(x_{1},t)g_{2}(x_{2},x_{3},t)

\displaystyle+f_{1}(x_{2},t)g_{2}(x_{1},x_{3},t)+f_{1}(x_{3},t)g_{2}(x_{1},x_{2},t)

(30)

\displaystyle+g_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},t)\,,

where
g_{r}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{r},t)
denotes the irreducible
r
-particle distribution function containing the subset of correlated particles within
f_{s}
. Importantly, this decomposition of the total phase space density
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
into reducible and irreducible
s
-particle distributions can be done in general at any time. Our approach described in the following will make use of it in order to decompose the initial phase space density into initial correlations, which are then propagated in time.

2.2
Time Evolution via Classical Path Integrals

In quantum mechanics, the intuitive picture behind the path integral representation of the transition probability is to allow the particles to travel all possible trajectories connecting two fixed end points in configuration space. Each trajectory is then functionally integrated over, weighted by the exponential of the classical action. This idea can be transferred to the classical situation as described in the following. To keep things short we mention the main ideas here and postpone a more detailed derivation and further discussion to appendix
A
and especially the literature
[
10
,
18
,
6
]
.

The classical transition probability (
13
) between an initial time
t^{(\mathrm{i})}
and a final time
t^{(\mathrm{f})}
satisfies the important relation

K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\mathrm{d}xK(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x},t)\,K(\boldsymbol{x},t|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

(31)

which can be used to decompose the evolution into
N
steps. The Dirac-Deltas then ensure that at every time step the particles can be found on the respective classical trajectories, which in the continuum limit
N\rightarrow\infty
converges to the classical trajectory and thus leads to the path integral representation of the classical transition amplitude,

K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{x}(t)-\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})]\,,

(32)

where the functional integration measure
\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{x}(t)-\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})]
gives weight
0
to all trajectories but the classical one, with fixed end points at
\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}
. This ensures classical determinism in the path integral approach.
We can now perform a transformation
\boldsymbol{x}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}[\boldsymbol{x}]
inside the functional Dirac-Delta distribution in order to explicitly use the equations of motion instead of the classical solution, which is not explicitly known in general. To this end we make use of the functional relation

\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{x}(t)-\boldsymbol{x}_{cl}(t;\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})]=\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t)]]\cdot\mathcal{J}\,,

(33)

where
\mathcal{J}=\left|\frac{\delta\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}[\boldsymbol{x}]}{\delta\boldsymbol{x}}\right|
is the Jacobian emerging from the change of variables. In appendix
A
we compute the Jacobian explicitly during the rigorous discretization procedure and discuss how its explicit form depends on the convention for the discretization. Furthermore, we show that by using the pre-point prescription in the discretized theory which corresponds to defining
\Theta(0)=0
, the determinant is constant and can be absorbed in the functional integration measure. This phenomenon is also known from the theory of stochastic dynamics and has been discussed in great detail in the literature
[
19
,
20
]
. Keeping this in mind, we plug (
33
) into (
32
), absorb the Jacobian and introduce a doublet
\boldsymbol{\chi}=(\boldsymbol{\chi_{q}},\boldsymbol{\chi_{p}})^{\top}
in order to express the remaining Dirac-Delta distribution in terms of its Fourier transform. We then arrive at the expression

K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}t\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\omega\cdot\nabla}H(\boldsymbol{x}(t))\right)\Bigg]\,,

(34)

where we explicitly inserted the equations of motion (
5
). Note that the functional integration is performed over all
\boldsymbol{x}(t)
trajectories with fixed endpoints and over all
\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)
trajectories with no restrictions on the end points. The representation (
34
) for the classical transition probability together with (
12
) opens the possibility for a functional approach to the time evolution of classical
N
-particle systems in and out of equilibrium, depending on the explicit form of the initial phase space density
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
. The out of equilibrium case will be the main goal of our application.

As is well known, the time evolution of a classical statistical system is governed by the
Liouville
equation,

\partial_{t}\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\,-\mathrm{i}\hat{L}\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\,,

(35)

where the Liouville operator is defined by

\displaystyle\hat{L}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\nabla}H(\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla}=\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\nabla_{q}}H(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla_{p}}-\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\nabla_{p}}H(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla_{q}}\,.

(36)

The formal solution to (
35
) is then given by

\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\hat{U}(t,t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(37)

with the classical time evolution operator
1
1

1

Here we assume a time independent Hamilton function for simplicity.

\hat{U}(t,t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t-t^{(\mathrm{i})})\hat{L}}\,.

(38)

In fact, as has been shown in
[
4
,
10
]
, (
34
) corresponds to the path integral
representation
of the time evolution operator (
38
). Indeed, expanding (
34
) for small time steps, one recovers the Liouville equation (
35
). Thus, it is the functional formulation of the associated operator formalism famously known as the Koopman-von Neumann formalism
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, which describes classical mechanics as an operatorial theory on the Hilbert space of complex square-integrable classical wavefunctions, in complete analogy to quantum mechanics. As is clear form the exponent in (
34
),
\boldsymbol{\chi}
plays the role of the conjugate momenta to
\boldsymbol{x}
in an extended phase space
[
10
]
. With that intuition in mind we finally note that the whole path integral construction is very similar to the one used in stochastic dynamics describing the Brownian motion of a particle subject to a Langevin equation
[
24
]
. However, in contrast to the latter case, we do not have a noise term spoiling the determinism of the time evolution. The only probabilistic element in our system enters through the initial phase space density distribution. It is thus the initial state which describes the statistical properties of the system, and thus determines whether it is in an equilibrium state or not.

From the path integral representation of the transition probability we can now construct analogous expressions for time ordered expectation values (
17
) in the following way: We insert the observables inside the path integration which enforces the microscopic classical trajectories. Furthermore, we integrate out the final microscopic degrees of freedom
\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}
and formally let
t_{f}\rightarrow\infty
, which leaves us with

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{O}_{1}(t_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(t_{k})\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\times

(39)

\displaystyle\times\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\mathcal{O}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}(t_{1}))\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}(t_{k}))\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]\,.

The effective classical action is given by

\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\Big(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\omega\cdot\nabla}H(\boldsymbol{x}(t))\Big)\,,

(40)

which, inserting (
1
), can be split into a free and an interacting part

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]+\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\,,

(41)

with

\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\Big\{\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\Big(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\omega\cdot\nabla}H_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}(t))\Big)\Big\}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\boldsymbol{\chi_{p}}(t)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla_{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q}(t),t)\,.

(42)

There are several ways to proceed with the computation of (
39
). The most straight forward way is to introduce sources for
\boldsymbol{q}
and
\boldsymbol{\chi_{p}}
and to pull the interactions and the operators out of the path integral by replacing the variables with appropriate functional derivatives. This has been done in
[
25
,
15
,
11
]
and results in a perturbation theory in terms of the interaction potential, leading to particle trajectories which deviate from their inertial free motion governed by
H_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})
. If the interactions are weak, the full trajectory can be approximated very accurately by low orders of the perturbative expansion. If, however, the operators one is interested in are non-polynomial functions of the underlying degrees of freedom
\boldsymbol{x}(t)
, this approach requires Taylor expanding these functions, introducing a further approximation and consequently a loss of accuracy. In the next section we therefore take a different path which, as we will see, is more closely related to standard kinetic theory and exhibits similarities to many-body quantum mechanics, such that it eventually results in a better approximation of the time evolution of the density.

2.3
Microscopic Perturbation Theory

In this section we describe the perturbative approach to the computation of expectation values. Without loss of generality we assume that the times of the operators are ordered,
t^{(\mathrm{f})}>t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{k}\geq t^{(\mathrm{i})}
. As described in appendix
A.1
, the path integral may then be split at the respective points
\boldsymbol{x}(t_{k})\equiv\boldsymbol{x}_{k}
, i. e.

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\mathcal{O}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}(t_{1}))\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}(t_{k}))\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]=

(43)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{k}\,K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1})\mathcal{O}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})K(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{x}_{2},t_{2})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})K(\boldsymbol{x}_{k},t_{k}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(44)

Note that the integration
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{k}
signifies integration over the whole ensemble at time
t_{k}
. Furthermore, due to the full propagator being a Dirac-Delta distribution of the classical trajectories (
13
), we find

1=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1})\,,

(45)

such that we can integrate out
\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}
in (
39
) which thus leaves us with

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{O}_{1}(t_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(t_{k})\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{k}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\mathcal{O}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})K(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{x}_{2},t_{2})\cdots

(46)

\displaystyle\cdots K(\boldsymbol{x}_{k-1},t_{k-1}|\boldsymbol{x}_{k},t_{k})\mathcal{O}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{k})K(\boldsymbol{x}_{k},t_{k}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

Equation (
46
) is a direct generalization of (
15
). We evolve the initial density with the full propagator until we take the first average at time
t_{k}
by integrating over the ensemble
\boldsymbol{x}_{k}
. The density is then further evolved until we take the next average over
\boldsymbol{x}_{k-1}
at
t_{k-1}
. This procedure is repeated up to
t_{1}
where we take the last average over
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
giving the full unequal-time correlation between the observables
\mathcal{O}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,\mathcal{O}_{k}
. The microscopic perturbation theory now consists
of expanding the full propagator (
34
) in a Dyson series and sorting the resulting terms in (
46
) by powers of the potential. Let us therefore write

\displaystyle K(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]+\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]

(47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{n}}{n!}\Big(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Big)^{n}

(48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}K_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(49)

In the following we occasionally abbreviate

K_{n}(k|l)\equiv K_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{k},t_{k}|\boldsymbol{x}_{l},t_{l})\,,\,\,\,\,\,K_{n}(f|i)\equiv K_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

in order to condense the notation. We easily find the zeroth order propagator,

\displaystyle K_{0}(f|i)=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[i\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]

(50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{D}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}-\boldsymbol{x}_{cl,0}(t^{(\mathrm{f})};\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})\right)

(51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{D}\left(\boldsymbol{q}^{(\mathrm{f})}-\boldsymbol{q}^{(\mathrm{i})}-\frac{\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{i})}}{m}(t^{(\mathrm{f})}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})\right)\delta_{D}\left(\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{f})}-\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,,

(52)

describing the free evolution of the particles. In order to restrict ourselves to causal propagation, we multiply the free propagator with
\Theta(t^{(\mathrm{f})}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})
such that

\displaystyle K_{0}(f|i)=\delta_{D}\left(\boldsymbol{q}^{(\mathrm{f})}-\boldsymbol{q}^{(\mathrm{i})}-\frac{\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{i})}}{m}(t^{(\mathrm{f})}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})\right)\delta_{D}\left(\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{f})}-\boldsymbol{p}^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,\Theta(t^{(\mathrm{f})}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

(53)

i. e. the density evolves from earlier to later times. Inserting the explicit form of the two particle potential (
2
) into
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]
we find for
K_{1}

\displaystyle K_{1}(f|i)=\mathrm{i}\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]\Bigg]\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\vec{\chi}_{p_{i}}(\bar{t})\cdot\nabla_{q_{i}}v(|\vec{q}_{i}(\bar{t})-\vec{q}_{j}(\bar{t})|,\bar{t})\,.

(54)

In appendix
A.1
we show that this expression can be brought into the form

\displaystyle K_{1}(f|i)=\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}\,K_{0}(f|\bar{1})\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{1})\,K_{0}(\bar{1}|i)\,,

(55)

where the potential operator
\hat{V}(1)
defined as

\hat{\mathcal{V}}(1)\equiv\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1})\equiv\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\nabla_{\vec{q}_{1_{i}}}v(|\vec{q}_{1_{i}}-\vec{q}_{1_{j}}|,t_{1})\cdot\nabla_{\vec{p}_{1_{i}}}

(56)

acts on the free propagator on its right and describes the reaction of the system to inter particle interactions. Again, we recall that
x_{1_{i}}
refers to the
i
-th particle at time
t_{1}
. To second order we analogously find

\displaystyle K_{2}(f|i)=\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\bar{t}_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{2}\,\int\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}\,K_{0}(f|\bar{1})\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{1})\,K_{0}(\bar{1}|\bar{2})\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{2})K_{0}(\bar{2}|i)\,.

(57)

Attention has to be paid to the time ordering, which we have made explicit in the boundaries of the time integration. In general there are
n!
possibilities of ordering
n
interactions, which exactly cancels the
\frac{1}{n!}
appearing in the expansion of the Dyson Series (
47
). Iterating the above procedure, we eventually arrive at the familiar integral equation for the full propagator

\displaystyle K(f|i)=K_{0}(f|i)+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}K_{0}(f|\bar{1})\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{1})\,K(\bar{1}|i)\,.

(58)

If we multiply (
58
) with the initial phase space density distribution and integrate over the initial state, we find the following
Lippmann-Schwinger
like equation for the full phase space density

\displaystyle\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})})=\varrho_{N}^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})})+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}K_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\bar{t}_{1})\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\bar{t}_{1})\,\varrho_{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\bar{t}_{1})\,,

(59)

where
\varrho_{N}^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})})
is the freely evolving density. Equation (
59
) describes how the full final density arises as a scattering process
2
2

2

The analogy with the scattering theory of many-body quantum mechanics is not a coincidence. In quantum mechanics the Lippmann-Schwinger equation describes a scattering process of a wave function with a potential w. r. t. a free evolution. Famously, classical mechanics ca be formulated within the same framework as quantum mechanics, know as Koopman-von Neumann formalism
[
21
]
. Within this framework it can be shown that a postulated classical wavefunction obeys the same evolution equation as the classical phase space density. Thus the scattering process described by the wave function translates to a scattering process of the density.
in which the incoming full phase space density is deformed by the interaction operator
\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
due to the presence of inter particle interactions. The first order approximation of this equation thus corresponds to the analogy of the Born approximation in scattering theory. Importantly, integrating (
59
) over the full final phase space, and using the normalization of the free and the full phase space density, we find the condition

\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}\,\hat{\mathcal{V}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\bar{t}_{1})\,\varrho_{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\bar{t}_{1})=0\,,

(60)

from which we obtain the normalization condition

\int\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,\nabla_{\vec{q}_{i}}v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|,t)\cdot\nabla_{\vec{p}_{i}}\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=0\,,

(61)

which also holds order by order in perturbation theory. It can also be verified by partially integrating over the momenta
\vec{p}_{i}
and using the fact, that the density vanishes at the boundaries in momentum space. Thus, all terms vanish in which the momentum of particle
i
, affected by the force induced by particle
j
, is integrated over. This normalization argument will be used in the following in order to find all contributing terms in perturbation theory.

Keeping the above in mind, we can further simplify the integrals over
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
in (
55
) and (
57
), respectively. Performing the integrals over the Dirac-Delta distributions, we find

\displaystyle K_{1}(1|2)=

\displaystyle\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\nabla_{\vec{q}_{1_{i}}}v\left(\left|\vec{q}_{1_{i}}(\bar{t}_{1};t_{1})-\vec{q}_{1_{j}}(\bar{t}_{1};t_{1})\right|,\bar{t}_{1}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\vec{p}_{1_{i}}}\,K_{0}(1|2)

(62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{i\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{}{}{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}j}(t_{1},t_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,K_{0}(1|2)\,,

(63)

where we introduced the short-hand notation

\vec{{q}}_{1}(t^{\prime};t_{1})=\vec{{q}}_{1}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{\prime})\,,

(64)

describing the history of a freely evolving particle as seen from
{x}_{1}=(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1})
at time
t_{1}
and also defined the interaction operator

\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{i\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}j}(t_{1},t_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\equiv\nabla_{\vec{q}_{1_{i}}}v\left(\left|\vec{q}_{1_{i}}(\bar{t}_{1};t_{1})-\vec{q}_{1_{j}}(\bar{t}_{1};t_{1})\right|,\bar{t}_{1}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\vec{p}_{1_{i}}}\,.

(65)

The latter describes how particle
j
exhibits a force on particle
i
at time
\bar{t}_{1}
. Both particles positions are described by their backwards evolution as seen from
t_{1}
. As expected,
K_{1}
describes the deviation from the free density propagation due to the interaction between
i
and
j
integrated over all possible times
\bar{t}_{1}
. The corresponding expression for
K_{2}(1|2)
reads

\displaystyle K_{2}(1|2)=\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int_{t_{2}}^{\bar{t}_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{2}\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{i\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}j}(t_{1},t_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,\left[\sum_{k\in\{i,j\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}l}(t_{1},t_{1};\bar{t}_{2})\right]\,K_{0}(1|2)\,,

(66)

and describes how a second interaction operator deforms the propagation of the initial density which has already been deformed by an interaction at earlier times. Importantly, the particle with index
k
, which is deflected in the earlier interaction has to be either the deflected particle
i
or the deflecting particle
j
in the later interaction. This is again due to the normalization condition (
61
). The same logic applies to all higher orders.

As an illustrative example we shall present the computations of the Klimontovich phase space density correlation functions

\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle
and
\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\rangle
up to second order in the perturbation theory.

2.3.1
Free Theory

In the simplest case we only have free propagators appearing. The expectation value of the Klimontovich phase space density at time
t_{1}
is obtained by averaging over the ensemble
\boldsymbol{x}(t_{1})\equiv\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
at the respective time, i. e.

\displaystyle\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})K_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(67)

Inserting the definitions (
18
) and (
53
) of the Klimontovich density and the free propagator, we easily compute

\displaystyle\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}_{i}\delta_{D}\left(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}_{i}-\frac{\vec{p}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}_{i}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})\right)\delta_{D}(\vec{{p}}_{1}-\vec{p}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}_{i})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}\left(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{1},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)

(69)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1})\,,

(70)

where in the first step we inserted the definition of the one-particle reduced initial phase space density (
24
) and in the last line we defined the freely evolved one-particle reduced distribution function. The final result is therefore, as expected, simply the shifted initial phase space density. A similar computation for
\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\rangle
reveals

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\rangle^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\,K_{0}(1|2)\,f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\,K_{0}(2|i)\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{D}({x}_{2}-{x}_{1}(t_{2};t_{1}))f_{1}^{(0)}({x}_{1},t_{1})+f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})\,,

(72)

which corresponds to (
27
) in the case of the free theory case with unequal times where

f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})=f_{2}\left(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{1},\vec{{q}}_{2}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{2}}{m}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{2},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,

(73)

is the freely evolved unequal-time generalization of
f_{2}
. Only the initial reduced phase space densities
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
appear in (
70
) and (
72
), respectively, since the free evolution does not add any further correlations to the ones already present at initial time. Thus, the result is, as expected, simply the initial correlation function shifted by the free trajectories. For the free theory we can generalize the result to
n
-th order correlation functions and find

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(\vec{{q}}_{n},\vec{{p}}_{n},t_{n})\rangle^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\cdots+f_{n}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},\cdots,x_{n},t_{n})\,,

(74)

where again the ellipsis stands for all lower-order particle contributions. Furthermore, the freely evolved
k
-particle reduced distribution function is, in analogy to (
73
), given by

f_{k}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},\cdots,x_{k},t_{k})=f_{2}\left(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{{q}}_{k}-\frac{\vec{{p}}_{k}}{m}(t_{k}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{k},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,,

(75)

where each particle is shifted individually.

2.3.2
First and Second Order Perturbation Theory

The first order correction to
\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle
now includes an interaction operator (
65
) and can be computed using (
62
) as

\displaystyle\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\,f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})K_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,

(76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(x_{1}-x_{1_{i}})\sum_{j\neq l=1}^{N}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{j\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}l}(t_{1},t_{1};\bar{t}_{1})K_{0}(1|i)\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}{x}_{2}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{1}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{2}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,\left[f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},\bar{t}_{1})\right]\,.

(78)

In the second equality we inserted the definitions of the Klimontovich phase space density. From the second to the third line we made use of the normalization condition (
61
) to identify the particle indices
j
with
i
and performed the remaining integrals over the Dirac-Delta distributions. Furthermore, we substituted
x_{1,l}(\bar{t}_{1};t_{1})
by
x_{2}
, such that the interaction operator now has the indices
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. The interpretation of the result is clear: A freely evolved two-particle distribution connecting the phase space points
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
is deformed by the force acting from
x_{2}
on
x_{1}
. The result is then averaged over
x_{2}
to yield the first-order correction to the mean density at
x_{1}
. Note, that the two-particle distribution
f_{2}
evolves
x_{2}
until it interacts at time
\bar{t}_{1}
, while the density at
x_{1}
is evolved to the final time
t_{1}
.

The first order correction to
\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\rangle
comes from the following two terms,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\rangle^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\,K_{1}(1|2)\,f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\,K_{0}(2|i)\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(79)

\displaystyle+\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\,K_{0}(1|2)\,f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})\,K_{1}(2|i)\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

since both terms come with a single power of the interaction potential. The first term describes an interaction taking place after the first average over
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
has been taken, while the second term describes the interaction even before the first averaging. Note that in the first term, the interaction operator again enforces, by the normalization argument (
61
), that the particle deflected by the potential corresponds to the particle contributing to the density
f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})
, while in the second term, the deflected particle can either be the particle contributing to
f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})
or to
f(\vec{{q}}_{2},\vec{{p}}_{2},t_{2})
. Nicely, both contributions to the deflection of the particle belonging to
f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})
add up to a single time integration over the whole time interval. Thus, upon performing all of the remaining integrals over the free propagators as above, we get the result

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f({x}_{1},t_{1})f({x}_{2},t_{2})\rangle^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\,\delta_{D}({x}_{2}-{x}_{1}(t_{2};t_{1}))\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}{x}_{3}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{1}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{3}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{3},\bar{t}_{1})

\displaystyle+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{1}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2};\bar{t}_{1})\,f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})

\displaystyle+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{2}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{1}}(t_{2},t_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,f_{2}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})

(80)

\displaystyle+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}{x}_{3}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{1}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{3}}(t_{1},\bar{t}_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,f_{3}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2},x_{3},\bar{t}_{1})

\displaystyle+\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\,\int\mathrm{d}{x}_{3}\,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{2}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{3}}(t_{2},\bar{t}_{1};\bar{t}_{1})\,f_{3}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2},x_{3},\bar{t}_{1})\,.

The first term is the first order correction to the one-particle contribution to the two point density correlation. The second and third terms are contributions in which densities at
\vec{{q}}_{1}
and
\vec{{q}}_{2}
influence each other respectively, while the last two terms represent contributions in which an external densities at
\vec{{q}}_{3}
influences the densities at
\vec{{q}}_{1}
and
\vec{{q}}_{2}
. See Fig.
1
and Fig.
2
for a diagrammatic representation of the first order corrections to
\langle f({x}_{1},t_{1})\rangle^{(1)}
and
\langle f({x}_{1},t_{1})f({x}_{2},t_{2})\rangle^{(1)}
.

Last but not least, we present the second order corrections to the mean density. Using (
66
) we find by the same computations as above

\displaystyle\langle f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})\rangle^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\,f(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})K_{2}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})

(81)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{1}\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\bar{t}_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}_{2}\int\mathrm{d}{x}_{2}\mathrm{d}{x}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x_{1}\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
