\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime};t^{\prime})
operators, where
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
belong to a response field. In order to avoid the repeated tedious computations it is convenient to summarize the form of
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{r,s^{\prime}}
on a diagrammatic level by the following set of rules:

1.

Draw one filled circle for every
f
and one empty circle for every
\mathcal{B}
appearing in the cumulant and label them with their respective phase space arguments. For a
G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\cdots X_{s}^{\prime})
cumulant we draw

X_{1}
\cdots
X_{r}
X_{1}^{\prime}
\cdots
X_{s}^{\prime}

(263)

2.

Start exactly one line at each empty circle which can end at any of the filled circles. Multiple lines can end on a filled circle. Note that not all of the filled circles have to have a line ending on them.

3.

A path segment connecting two circles
X
X^{\prime}
, corresponds to a
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{x\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime};t^{\prime})
operator, where the arrow signifies the causal flow due to the Heaviside function inside
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
.

(264)

Note, that if multiple operators end on the same position, the product rule applies. However the order of the operators does not matter. In the above example, the first operator acts also on the second. We could also have switched both giving the same result.

5.

All possible diagrams for given
r
,
s^{\prime}
are summed up to obtain
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{r,s^{\prime}}
.

6.

If there is no
\mathcal{B}
field involved,
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{r,s^{\prime}}=1

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x_{4}}(t_{2},t_{4};t_{4})\right]

\displaystyle\cdot G_{ff}^{(0)}(X_{1},X_{2})

Clearly, the higher order diagrams represent interactions, which deform higher, freely evolved phase space statistics multiple times. The last cumulant for instance describes how the connected two-point statistic can be affected by two interactions.

D.2
Density and Momentum Cumulants for Homogeneous System

In this appendix, we list the cumulants for the homogeneous system which contribute to the tree level expectation values of (
152
). They are computed as described in the previous section.

\displaystyle G_{\rho}^{(0)}(t_{1})=

\displaystyle\,\bar{\rho}

(271)

\displaystyle\vec{G}_{\Pi}^{(0)}(t_{1})=

\displaystyle 0

(272)

\displaystyle G_{\rho\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{2}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\,\Delta_{\vec{X}}\,v(|\vec{X}|,t_{2})\,\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)\,,

(273)

\displaystyle\vec{G}_{\Pi\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{3}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\,\Big[(\vec{R}-\vec{X})\Delta_{\vec{X}}\,v(|\vec{X}|,t_{2})-\nabla_{\vec{X}}v(|\vec{X}|,t_{2})\Big]\,\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)\,,

(274)

\displaystyle G_{\rho\rho}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{3}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{3}}\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)

(275)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{6}}{(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{3}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\mathrm{d}^{3}Yg_{2}\left(\vec{Y},\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,\frac{\vec{Y}-\vec{X}}{t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)

\displaystyle\vec{G}_{\Pi\rho}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{4}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{4}}\vec{R}\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)

(276)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{7}}{(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{4}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\mathrm{d}^{3}Y(\vec{R}-\vec{X})g_{2}\left(\vec{Y},\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,\frac{\vec{Y}-\vec{X}}{t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)

\displaystyle\vec{G}_{\rho\Pi}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{4}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{4}}\vec{R}\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)

(277)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{7}}{(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{3}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\mathrm{d}^{3}Y(\vec{Y}-\vec{X})g_{2}\left(\vec{Y},\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,\frac{\vec{Y}-\vec{X}}{t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)

\displaystyle G_{\Pi\otimes\Pi}^{(0)}(\vec{R},t_{1},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}m^{5}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{5}}\vec{R}\otimes\vec{R}\varphi\left(\frac{\vec{R}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}m\right)

(278)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{8}}{(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{4}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})})^{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}X\mathrm{d}^{3}Y(\vec{R}-\vec{X})\otimes(\vec{Y}-\vec{X})\times

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times g_{2}\left(\vec{Y},\frac{\vec{R}-\vec{X}}{t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,\frac{\vec{Y}-\vec{X}}{t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}m,t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)

(279)

where for
\vec{G}_{\Pi}^{(0)}
we made the reasonable assumption that that the mean momentum of the homogeneous system vanishes. For
G_{\rho\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
and
\vec{G}_{\Pi\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
we performed a partial integration.

Appendix E
Special Case: Field Theory Approach to Self Gravitating System in and out of Equilibrium

We will use the explicit example of a self-gravitating system to provide some physical intuition for the newly constructed field theory in Sec.
3.1
. This special case of our theory is a generalisation of
[
27
]
, where the HST has been used to study a self-gravitating gas in terms of a grand canonical equilibrium system.

We consider the Hamilton function (
1
) and assume throughout this subsection an explicit two-particle interaction of the form,

v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|,t)=\,m_{i}\,m_{j}\,v_{G}(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|)\,,\,\,\,\,v_{G}(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|)=-\frac{G}{|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{q}_{j}|}\,,

(280)

namely the gravitational potential. We introduce the doublet

\Phi(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})=(B_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1}),\,\,\rho_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1}))^{\top}\,,

(281)

with the particle mass density
\rho_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})
and the response field
B_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})
defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))

(282)

\displaystyle B_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})=

\displaystyle-i\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\vec{\chi}_{p_{i}}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{{q}}_{1}}\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{q}_{i}(t_{1}))\,.

(283)

Note that contrary to the response field introduced in Sec.
3.1
,
B_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}_{1})
does not contain the interaction potential.
In analogy to (
84
), we bring the interaction part of the action (
41
) into a quadratic form,

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\,\mathcal{S}_{I}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\boldsymbol{\chi}_{p}(t)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla_{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q}(t))

(284)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}^{3}{q}_{1}\int\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}^{3}{q}_{2}\,B_{m}(t_{1},{q}_{1})\,v_{G}(|{q}_{1}-{q}_{2}|)\delta_{D}(t_{1}-t_{2})\,\rho_{m}(t_{2},{q}_{2})

(285)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}Q_{1}\int\mathrm{d}Q_{2}\,\Phi(Q_{1})^{\top}\cdot\sigma(Q_{1},Q_{2})\cdot\Phi(Q_{2})\;.

(286)

where in the last line we abbreviated the notation by writing

\mathrm{d}Q_{n}\equiv\mathrm{d}t_{n}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{{q}}_{n}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,A(t_{n},\vec{{q}}_{n})\equiv A(Q_{n})\,,

(287)

and defined the interaction matrix

\sigma(Q_{1},Q_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma_{B\rho}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\\
\sigma_{\rho B}(Q_{1},Q_{2})&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(288)

with

\sigma_{B\rho}(Q_{1},Q_{2})=\sigma_{\rho B}(Q_{2},Q_{1})=v_{G}(|\vec{q}_{1}-\vec{q}_{2}|)\delta_{D}(t_{1}-t_{2})\,.

(289)

Introducing a source doublet

\mathcal{J}(Q_{1})=(J_{\rho}(Q_{1}),\,\,J_{B}(Q_{1})^{\top}\,,

(290)

we can define the macroscopic generating functional in complete analogy to (
93
),
such that by construction functional derivatives w. r. t.
J_{\rho}(Q)
and
J_{B}(Q)
produce integrals over the interaction potential and density and response field correlators,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{i\delta J_{\rho}(Q_{1})}\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]\Big\rvert_{J_{\rho},J_{B}=0}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{{q}}^{\prime}v_{G}(|\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}^{\prime}|)\langle\rho_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}^{\prime})\rangle

(291)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{i\delta J_{B}(Q_{1})}\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]\Big\rvert_{J_{\rho},J_{B}=0}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{{q}}^{\prime}v_{G}(|\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}^{\prime}|)\langle B_{m}(t_{1},\vec{{q}}^{\prime})\rangle\,,

(292)

which continues analogously for higher order and mixed correlation functions. In order to solve for the density and response field correlators in (
291
) and (
292
), the gravitational interaction potential (
280
) has to be inverted by means of its Green function, the Laplace operator,

\frac{1}{4\pi G}\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\,v_{G}(|\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}_{2}|)=\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}_{2})\,.

(293)

This allows us to directly compute correlations of the density and response field as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi G}\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\frac{\delta}{i\delta J_{\rho}(Q_{1})}\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]\Big\rvert_{J_{\rho},J_{B}=0}=\langle\rho_{m}(Q_{1})\rangle

(294)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi G}\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\frac{\delta}{i\delta J_{B}(Q_{1})}\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]\Big\rvert_{J_{\rho},J_{B}=0}=\langle B_{m}(Q_{1})\rangle\,,

(295)

which can be generalised to higher order correlation functions in a straightforward fashion.
We now apply the HST and follow the steps (
97
) to (
103
) using that the inverse of
\sigma(Q_{1},Q_{2})
is given by

\sigma^{-1}(Q_{1},Q_{2})=\delta_{D}(t_{1}-t_{2})\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}_{2})\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{4\pi G}\Delta_{q_{1}}\\
\frac{1}{4\pi G}\Delta_{q_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(296)

After the HST, the generating functional takes on the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi\exp\Bigg[

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}Q_{1}\mathrm{d}Q_{2}\Psi(Q_{1})^{\top}\cdot\sigma^{-1}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\cdot\Psi(Q_{2})

\displaystyle+\int\mathrm{d}Q_{1}\mathcal{J}(Q_{1})^{\top}\cdot\Psi(Q_{1})\Bigg]\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{\rho B}\big[\Psi_{B},\Psi_{\rho}\big]\;,

(297)

where we defined,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{\rho B}\big[\Psi_{B},\Psi_{\rho}\big]

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}\varrho_{N}(t^{(\mathrm{i})},\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})})\times

\displaystyle\times\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[i\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}(t)]+\int\mathrm{d}1\Psi(Q_{1})^{\top}\cdot\Phi(Q_{1})\Bigg]\,.

(298)

The absence of an interaction term in (
E
) makes it clear that
\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{\rho B}\big[\Psi_{B},\Psi_{\rho}\big]
corresponds to the free
\rho B
-generating functional, whose functional derivatives w. r. t.
\Psi_{B}
and
\Psi_{\rho}
produce free
\rho B
-correlation functions,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{r+s}}{\delta\Psi_{B}(Q_{1})\cdots\Psi_{B}(Q_{r})\Psi_{\rho}({Q_{1}}^{\prime})\cdots\Psi_{\rho}({Q_{s}}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{\rho B}\big[\Psi_{B},\Psi_{\rho}\big]\Big\rvert_{\Psi=0}

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{m}(Q_{1})\cdots\rho_{m}(Q_{r})B_{m}({Q_{1}}^{\prime})\cdots B_{m}({Q_{s}}^{\prime})\rangle^{(0)}\,.

(299)

The physical interpretation of the macroscopic fields can be seen as follows: The expectation value of
\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{1})
can be computed as usual by taking a functional derivative,

\langle\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{1})\rangle=\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{\rho}(Q_{1})}\mathcal{Z}[J_{\rho},J_{B}]\Big\rvert_{J_{\rho},J_{B}=0}\,.

(300)

From (
294
) we thus find

\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\langle\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{1})\rangle=4\pi G\langle\rho_{m}(Q_{1})\rangle

(301)

which is the Poisson equation. Thus
\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{1})
corresponds to the macroscopic gravitational field induced by the microscopic 2-particle interactions of the ensemble. The macroscopic correlators are thus correlation functions of the gravitational field itself and we find in general the correspondence

\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\cdots\Delta_{{q}_{n}}\langle\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{1})\cdots\Psi_{\rho}(Q_{n})\rangle=(4\pi G)^{n}\langle\rho_{m}(Q_{1})\cdots\rho_{m}(Q_{n})\rangle\;.

(302)

Analogous equations can be derived for the macroscopic field
\Psi_{B}(Q_{1})
which encodes the macroscopic response of the system to fluctuations of the gravitational field. As expected,

\Delta_{{q}_{1}}\cdots\Delta_{{q}_{n}}\langle\Psi_{B}(Q_{1})\cdots\Psi_{B}(Q_{n})\rangle=0\;,

(303)

due to
\langle B_{m}(Q_{1})\cdots B_{m}(Q_{n})\rangle=0
.
Consequently, the HST has inverted the scale on which we describe our system: Instead of microscopic particle dynamics, we now describe the dynamics of a fluctuating macroscopic field whose self-interactions are sourced by the underlying microscopic free statistics.It is worth noting that in this special case, due to the functional inversion of the interaction potential, we were able to split the generating functional (
E
) into a part containing the full interaction potential
\Psi(Q_{1})^{\top}\cdot\sigma^{-1}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\cdot\Psi(Q_{2})
and a free part (
E
) containing no effects of the interaction potential at all. A very similar procedure has led
[
27
]
to a field theoretic description of a self gravitating gas in equilibrium. In our case however, we are not restricted to the equilibrium case, as we may put the system initially in any desired state. The free correlators will then transfer the initial information into the macroscopic field dynamics. Last but not least, we note that the above procedure can be applied to different two-particle potentials, provided, that there exists an appropriate differential operator of which the potential is the Greens function (
293
). The physical interpretation of the macroscopic fields will change according to the potential.
